TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* China Takes Its Lawfare to Sea
  Foreign Policy | 27 Oct 04:01 UTC
  Beijing is making life difficult for Panamanian ships, and others could be next.
  FULL TEXT: For most of the world, the diplomatic spat between the United States and Panama—over U.S. President Donald Trump’s incorrect claim in his 2025 inaugural speech that China controlled the Panama Canal and that he would retake it—has been resolved. Following the president’s threat, the Trump administration negotiated access for U.S. military training in the Panamanian jungle and Panama’s Supreme Court overturned contracts for two Hong Kong-owned port facilities. For Panamanians, however, the dispute around the canal continues. Only the White House has moved on. For most of the world, the diplomatic spat between the United States and Panama—over U.S. President Donald Trump’s incorrect claim in his 2025 inaugural speech that China controlled the Panama Canal and that he would retake it—has been resolved. Following the president’s threat, the Trump administration negotiated access for U.S. military training in the Panamanian jungle and Panama’s Supreme Court overturned contracts for two Hong Kong-owned port facilities. For Panamanians, however, the dispute around the canal continues. Only the White House has moved on. Between March and July 2026, more than 400 Panama-flagged ships were detained at Chinese ports. The unprecedented rate in detentions began on March 8, reportedly following what the U.S. Federal Maritime Commission has called “informal directives” intended to “punish” Panama-flagged ships. (China’s ambassador to the United States has denied that Panamanian ships are being targeted and says that the increased inspections are due to fishing boat collisions.) By April, China was responsible for 91 percent of all detentions of Panama-flagged ships across the Asia-Pacific region. Fearing additional detentions and penalties from China, an estimated 500 ships left Panama’s registry between the end of 2025 and this August—about 12.3 percent of the country’s total fleet by tonnage. Though not yet an open trade war, Beijing’s recent actions against Panama signal a new way of punishing countries for crossing Chinese interests. Beijing is applying economic pressure through the infrastructure of trade: shipping, ports, and logistics. With the world focused on another maritime chokepoint , Panama is paying the price for angering Beijing. And as freedom of navigation is very much in the news , the implications are broader still. Lawfare now encompasses maritime lawfare as well, and Panama and the United States cannot address this issue alone. The China-Panama disp
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/27/panama-canal-china-maritime-lawfare/

  FULL TEXT: The stunning majority victory of the Student List in Serbia’s parliamentary elections on Oct. 25 was a vital reprieve for a beleaguered continent. As furious youth protests sweep across Europe, Gen Zers in Serbia have proved that youth can achieve transformative change through nonviolent political engagement. The extraordinary discipline, diligence, and organization exhibited by Serbian students over two pressure-filled years are worthy of a Nobel Peace Prize. But a student-led protest movement years in the making appears to be what finally upended Vucic’s grip on power. In November 2024, demonstrations erupted over the collapse of a concrete railway station canopy that killed 16 people. Activists accused Belgrade of negligence, corruption, and poor state oversight. Security forces responded with excessive force, arbitrary arrests, and digital repression. Still, the protest movement never died out, and on Sunday, the Student List group won 52.54 percent of the vote, with voter turnout hitting nearly 69 percent—a more than 30-year high. “It is clear that we have lost these elections,” Vucic said in a televised speech. Accusations that China is exporting excess capacity have echoed across the United States, the European Union, and Southeast Asia , though China has vehemently rejected this. The concerns are understandable: It is hard to compete with Chinese industrial might, and local industry stakeholders worry about everything from job losses to dependence on Chinese suppliers. But the conventional view that China’s dominant position in the global market is the result of the central government’s long-term strategic planning and top-down industrial policy tells only half the story. Beijing is not fully in control of the bulldozer of Chinese manufacturing, and the results have sometimes been far beyond what the government wanted or planned for. In 2004, for instance, when the State Council, China’s central government, announced the launch of the high-speed railway program, its original plan was to build 12,000 kilometers of track by 2020. China ended up with nearly 38,000 kilometers by the end of that year. The explanation lies at the local level. In broad strokes, there are three options. First, ramp the war back up. Some—including U.S. President Donald Trump—have floated the idea of returning to a full-scale bombing campaign similar to what we saw during the conflict’s initial phase. O thers have proposed escalating the war , for example by using ground forces to seize Iranian territory. At the other end of the spectrum lies a second option: The United States could walk away from the conflict. Even Republican supporters of the Trump administration have increasingly called for an end to the war. The third option is to stay the current course: Maintain the blockade and economic pressure campaign, respond with force when Iran or its proxies strike U.S. or partner targets, and push for negotiations that would reopen the Strait of Hormuz and end the Iranian nuclear program. Ricks wrote for this magazine in 2012, “Americans frankly don’t give a damn about foreign policy, Scarlett. It typically takes extraordinary circumstances for foreign policy to influence voting behavior, whether that concerns a full-scale mobilization, as in the 1942 and 1944 elections, or the deployment of large numbers of ground troops, as in the 1970 or 2006 elections. Yet the connection between military operations and economic conditions can also become politically potent. --- Editorial (36) ---

* You Finally Got a Wikipedia Page. Then It Vanished.
  Longreads | 26 Oct 18:32 UTC
  "Every day, Wikipedia biographies of living people are flagged for deletion because the subjects are not 'notable' enough. How do these poor souls take the news?"
  FULL TEXT: If there’s a Wikipedia page about yourself, you must be famous—some kind of celebrity—or an expert in your field, or a figure who’s made a prominent contribution to human history or culture. But what happens when that page is nominated by Wikipedia editors for deletion? In a quirky tech story for Wired , Duncan Fyfe reports on the strange experience of being told you’re just not notable enough. There is, when you look for it, a careless and cocky tone that can creep into the verdicts of Wikipedia editors who have been doing the job for a long time. “This guy is a nobody. Delete him.” “I’ve got more followers on Instagram than he does.” “All we have here are stand-up comedy, opinion columns, being a guest or collaborator on some non-notable podcasts … This is more or less every twentieth person who lives in NYC.” “Wikipedia and the problem of historical memory.” “A months-old subreddit called r/BoyDinnerDiares has drawn over 4 million users and become a place for men to spill their guts. Why there and why now?” “The famous subreddit started as a forum for one man to ask about his workplace behavior. Seven years later, it’s become a platform where millions of people discuss good, bad, and everything in between. How did it get here? And can it actually help make people better?” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014. More by Cheri Lucas Rowlands

* The Lucrative World of Literary Scams
  Longreads | 26 Oct 18:32 UTC
  "Why writers are the perfect target."
  FULL TEXT: When Snigdha Poonam received a strange email from an organization seemingly interested in discussing a book she published seven years ago, she was intrigued, but apprehensive. As it turns out, her suspicions were well-founded. Poonam, after investigating, discovered she’d been the target of an author scam. Little did the grifter know, that Poonam has spent the last 10 years reporting on fraud. Game on! This was undoubtedly a scam. Genuine book clubs don’t ask for money from authors. But who was behind it? Had these individuals actually read my work, they would have wanted to stay away from me. I have spent the last 10 years reporting on transnational fraud. My first book, Dreamers, closed with a chapter on fake call centers in India; my second, Scamlands, traveled to other low-income countries in Asia to portray an invisible empire that thrives on inequality, technological change and the erosion of trust. Along the way, I met and interviewed the scammers whose labor keeps this empire running. For some, fraud was a route to upward mobility; for others, an act of revenge against the privileged. Though I have formally emerged from this underworld, I can never delete a scam email without wanting to know more. “An ambitious young couple, a multimillion-dollar swindle and the sting that brought them down.” “For years, North Korea has been secretly placing young IT workers inside Western companies. With AI, their schemes are now more devious—and effective—than ever.” “How the RCMP busted the biggest fraud ever to target Canadians—and why they can’t keep up anymore.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/26/literary-scams-the-dial/

* Benchma(r)king
  Longreads | 26 Oct 14:00 UTC
  "James Bridle experiments with combining their practical carpentry skills and the abundant theoretical knowledge of AI to build a chair."
  FULL TEXT: For Emergence Magazine , James Bridle writes about artificial intelligence; carpentry and chair-making with ChatGPT; and Enzo Mari, a famed Italian furniture designer from the 1950s and ’60s. Bridle asks: “What if we used AI for something actually useful ?” They offer an interesting take on AI, and why the extractive way we’re using it is far from satisfying. What we have here is, quite literally, a chair designed by someone who has read a lot about chairs but never actually sat in one. They know what a chair should look like, what it should do, the mechanics of how one is put together, the physics of holding you up. But they have no embodied experience of the world, no history with chairs or any other object, no physical memory. They don’t know how it feels, not merely to sit in a chair, but to encounter one in the world, to place it among other things in a home, to live with it, to be around it every day as one goes about the mundane business of life. This is the idea of a chair brought to life; but things—objects, beings, relationships—are not mere ideas, and you certainly can’t get every aspect of them from books. The chair is low stakes, but, once again, you wouldn’t want to run a society, or a culture, or a planet this way. “I am a person of ground. This is not to say grounded, but of earth, attached to the earth.” “How can noticing seasonal changes be a radical act in a culture fixated with endless growth?” “From ochre to lapis lazuli, Stephanie Krzywonos opens a door into the entangled histories of our most iconic pigments, revealing how colors hold stories of both lightness and darkness.” “But here the line is blurred. In the afterlife of cedars, nothing is ever dead.” “Finding solace in the company of bees, Emily Polk opens to the widening circles of loss around her and an enduring spirit of survival.” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014. More by Cheri Lucas Rowlands
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/26/ai-intelligence-carpentry/

* Vocal Accords
  Longreads | 26 Oct 12:00 UTC
  "Eurovision faces the music."
  FULL TEXT: When Russia invaded Ukraine, it was summarily booted from one of the world’s greatest—or worst, depending on your taste—pop-culture celebrations: Eurovision. When Israel launched its genocidal campaign in Gaza, it was allowed to keep competing. And it’s done very well. Lisa Dorst goes into the pulsing belly of the gaudy Eurovision beast to interrogate the competition’s politics: One word that comes up a lot in attempts to describe the Eurovision aesthetic is “camp.” In a blog post for the London Review of Books, Andrew O’Hagan pronounced that Eurovision 2009 was “the biggest, campest carnival yet.” The following year, Anthony Lane, in The New Yorker, characterized the contest by its “reliable deployment of camp, whether sly or unwitting.” I’d brushed up on my Sontag before heading to Vienna and thought I’d prepared for artifice, for schlock, for the pileup of off-kilter theatricality and post-disco schmaltz that inspired Carl Wilson, in Let’s Talk About Love, to label Eurovision the “Cheeseball Olympics.” But as the hook to “Ferto” lodged itself further in my head and Eurovision’s physical presence continued to intrude, it was becoming harder to see all this as properly winking, witty, and “disengaged, depoliticized—or at least apolitical,” as Sontag puts it in her second Note on Camp. What was emanating from the Stadthalle seemed closer to “kitsch,” that nice German loanword for camp without wit. Kitsch is politicized and shaped by residual history; its precondition, Clement Greenberg wrote in 1939, is “the availability close at hand of a fully matured cultural tradition” whose familiar formulas could be recycled by reactionary regimes toward the aim of mass appeal. “From a certain height, the problem becomes one of knowledge itself: Do you believe what you hear, or do you hear what you believe?” “He was Queen’s flamboyant frontman. She was his shy girlfriend, dreaming of marriage until they both realised he preferred men.” “The fact that I had song ideas, let alone a place where I was saving them, was significant. It had been 15 years since I’d last released an album.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/26/eurovision-israel-genocide/

* AI models cost more when you don’t use English
  Rest of World | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Some languages require far more tokens for AI models to process, raising costs and reducing context for the people who use them.
  FULL TEXT: People who use less widely spoken languages could end up paying more to use artificial intelligence than those who speak English or other dominant languages. A new study published last month by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found AI models require far more tokens for some languages than others. Tokens are the units of text or data that the models process. The researchers have called this a hidden “tokenizer tax,” which can make AI more expensive in languages with smaller online presence. A tokenizer is a tool that breaks down raw text, images, or audio into tokens. Even within the 20 most popular languages on the internet, there is a gap in token usage, the research found. The most expensive of the languages used between 1.5 and 5 times more tokens than the cheapest. This gap widened when researchers expanded the set to 200 languages, with the token cost gap in one AI model growing by 17 times. “A startup in [Bengaluru] may pay a surcharge on every prompt and completion while its competitor in Austin does not,” the study said. “The tokenizer tax also has implications beyond cost. Higher token counts translate into higher latency and reduced effective context capacity. These penalties hit hardest where compute and connectivity are already limited. Tokenizer inequity is a constraint on who can participate in the AI economy.” Non-English languages leave less room for documents or conversation history, and require more work to generate the answer, according to Pratyush Choudhury, co-founder of Activate AI, India’s first venture capital fund dedicated exclusively to AI. Activate backs multilingual voice AI startups like ElevenLabs, Sarvam, and Wispr Flow. “Think about a tutor: Retaining the student’s earlier mistakes and explanations is part of delivering a good lesson. If that history consumes the context window much faster in one language, the disadvantage reaches into the quality of the service,” he said. One way to improve language processing is to improve the underlying data. “Open data efforts matter here, like RESPIN, a collaborative initiative with [the Indian Institute of Science] funded by the Gates Foundation, where we collected text from native speakers of 38 dialects along with read speech,” Raoul Nanavati, co-founder and CEO of Mumbai-based AI voice infrastructure startup Navana.ai , told Rest of World .   https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-tokenizer-tax-non-english-languages-cost-inequity/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

Four years later, in 1930, then-Prime Minister Taj al-Din al-Hasani, a turbaned cleric with a reformist agenda, granted the school a plot of land on Baghdad Street, to which it formally relocated in 1932. Laique quickly became one of the most prestigious institutions in Syria, celebrated for its coeducation, the quality of its faculty, and its tennis, volleyball and basketball courts. It also had an impressive library, along with a full-fledged theater that once hosted Egyptian diva Umm Kulthum in 1955. That colorful tapestry was part of a Middle East that imagined itself as part of a broader Arab world with a united identity. Fast forward 100 years, and Laique has become a shadow of its former self. First came 61 years of Baath Party rule that imposed an indoctrinating socialist curriculum on the entire Syrian school system, along with khaki uniforms. Teachers at Laique were hired not because they were good, but because they were Baathists. Then came the 1967 nationalization that ended the school’s autonomy and distorted its institutional identity, renaming it “Mahad al-Huriyya,” or the “Institute of Freedom,” a nod to the language of Baathism, with its three ideological pillars of freedom, pan-Arabism and socialism. The fact that Hafez al-Assad chose Laique for all his children — Bashar included — only hardened the school’s Baathism. When Assad’s eldest son and chosen successor, Bassel al-Assad, died in 1994, the school was renamed after him. What did not disappear was the memory of what Laique had been, and, more importantly, its alumni network of engineers, doctors, politicians and artists who became the “who’s who” of Syrian society. For decades, “I went to Laique” functioned as a kind of passport, akin to “I went to Brummana High School” in Lebanon: a signal of academic excellence that guaranteed entry into the workforce and came with high expectations of talent and character. * Traction!
  Aeon | 23 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Tribologists study friction: what happens when things rub together. It’s indispensable, from the wheel to the paths of stars - by Jennifer R Vail Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In conversations, few words catch my attention faster than ‘friction’ – and especially talk of making things frictionless . From the workplace to grocery shopping, our societies seem to be striving to remove friction from everything. The motivation appears to be that decreasing resistance makes life easier and smoother. Having spent a career as a tribologist, studying friction, wear and lubrication scientifically, I have some opinions on this. I’ll confess that, in most of my career, I too have sought to reduce friction, developing new materials, designs or even instrumentation in order to lower friction in a jet engine or reduce emissions from your car. However, I’ve also found myself trying to figure out how to increase friction to remove plaque on teeth. The reality is that friction, despite seeming so antagonistic, is necessary and even productive. The force that resists motion has, since humans first struck a spark from a stone, been the very thing that propels us forward. And this paradox offers a lens through which to reread humanity’s entire technological history. I n order to appreciate all that an understanding of friction has helped us to accomplish, it’s necessary to start at square one and state what friction actually is. We define friction as the force that resists motion, and that means friction is rather ubiquitous. If things are moving, whether they’re sliding around or rolling, there’s friction. Solids, liquids, gases? Doesn’t matter. If there’s movement, friction is there to resist it. Unfortunately, because friction’s job is to resist, we’ve developed an aversion to it as a nuisance. But it’s a very important force, and we’ve thrived by expanding our understanding of it. By the 1960s, after millennia of evolving with friction, we finally accepted that friction is such a critical part of our world that a scientific field of study needed to be devoted to it: tribology. Tribologists like me study this force in all its forms. Tribology is the science of interacting surfaces in relative motion, concerned with the wear of materials, friction and lubrication. Basically, tribology is the specialisation of things rubbing against each other. You may raise an eyebrow at a field of science focused on this, but when you pause to consider how much motion is going on inside us, around us and throughout the Universe, you can appreciate that tribology is vast. More than anyone, tribologists know the problems friction can cause. But they also know the ac
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-a-world-without-friction-is-a-world-without-progress

The late Middle Ages was a tough time to be alive, between the Black Death, various crusades and holy wars, and famine—not to mention the labor conditions of silver mining. Kutná Hora was also a popular place to die, and all the villagers wanted to spend eternity at the medieval Sedlec Abbey. After one of the crusades, an abbot had brought back a handful of soil, supposedly from Golgotha, and strewn it on the abbey grounds. Burial in that soil was believed to grant quicker access to heaven, which led to an overcrowded cemetery. The solution? Build a subterranean chapel to house the remains. And then a half-blind monk, in the wake of a terrible plague, exhumed the victims and used them to redecorate. An ossuary is any repository for human remains, but it’s also the term for a holy site that serves this function. Another name for this kind of church is charnel house, which to me evokes the image of an abattoir. Death historian Philippe Ariès connects the term to the French idiom une vieille carne , translated as an old nag or a tough piece of meat. * Big Tech’s thirst for water is sparking a global resistance
  Rest of World | 22 Oct 10:00 UTC
  This photograph taken on September 6, 2026 shows a placard opposing data centers displayed on a water dispenser during the People's Convention on AI Data Centers event at Visakhapatnam in India's Andhra Pradesh state. Construction of a massive Google data centre in the Indian city of Visakhapatnam, often called Vizag, has been met with re...
  FULL TEXT: After ChatGPT was released in November 2022, the tech industry was quick to herald the chatbot as the marker of a new wave of technological progress. As companies pushed to create more powerful AI models, the water demand of their ambitions took a while to become clear. In April 2023, a research paper out of the University of California estimated that training the large language model behind the initial version of ChatGPT had required 185,000 gallons of water. For every extended conversation someone had with the chatbot or every time they asked it to write an email, they were effectively pouring a bottle of water down the drain. That statistic got a lot of play when it was released. It became an early warning sign of what was coming as companies pushed chatbots as a universal technology that could replace psychologists, educators , lawyers, and even people’s friends or partners . As public concern grew, Google and OpenAI came out with their own figures. The companies suggested that querying one of their chatbots used mere drops of water. Independent researchers were not impressed, arguing the companies were taking a narrow view of the water use necessary to power and cool increasingly monstrous data centers. Those water demands can be quite significant too. A hyperscale data center can suck up between 1 and 5 million gallons of water a day, comparable to the amount used by a city of 10,000 to 50,000 people — depending on the part of the world we’re talking about. Consider how these companies often cluster data centers together and it becomes obvious what a challenge it can be to supply them. Data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. The water demands of individual data centers are also continuing to grow. It’s not just that they need more water for cooling as they become larger and contain more servers. The graphics processing units (GPUs) that power generative AI are much more resource-intensive than the chips and storage devices that traditionally filled data center racks. GPUs run harder and throw off more heat , meaning they also need more energy or water to keep them cool. Data centers cannot just use any kind of water either; it has to be clean enough to ensure the equipment and pipes are not damaged. Tapping into public drinking water is both easier and cheaper than paying the cost of treatment themselves. That means data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply.   https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-datacenters-water-resistance-global/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

Our little dyad, with dependants, was standing on its own feet, even if something felt decidedly unstable underneath. More space, more privacy, more control – all are sold as natural, unquestioned goods to be sought in the unconscious script that most of us carry. For the nuclear family, they are tokens of success and maturity, whereas forgoing these ‘goods’ and living with people you are neither shagging nor parenting has an association with low-status, studenty seasons. In particular, the film follows the life of a Kyiv native named Kseniia, who makes the difficult decision to send her children abroad as she stays in the country and runs a ‘humanitarian headquarters’ distributing donations to those in need. Through his portrait of the invasion from the perspective of Ukrainian civilians, Hammer explores how their lives are defined by horrific violence, enduring terror and ceaseless solidarity. * The Man Who Taught Us How to Pay Attention
  Longreads | 20 Oct 16:12 UTC
  "The works of the British art critic John Berger offer the antidote to our age of infinite scrolling."
  FULL TEXT: John Berger spent a meaningful portion of his life thinking, with precision and care, about how to look at the world—an effort he detailed in numerous essays and books, among them Ways of Seeing and About Looking . “Reading them, you often feel you’re standing next to him in the museum or even looking through his eyes at a painting or a sculpture,” writes A.O. Scott. “You pay attention to his attention.” Scott, an attentive reader himself , brings Berger’s lifelong project into our famously distractable era; his consideration of Berger is a primer for observation and study, a gentle nudge to focus, lest we lose sight of something essential. Right now, we’re in the midst of an attention crisis, individually and collectively snared in an infinite scroll of disruption and distraction. Berger saw this crisis coming and foresaw its consequences. In 1978, writing about photography and its impact on the modern world, he argued that the dominance of images “creates an eternal present of immediate expectation: Memory ceases to be necessary or desirable. With the loss of memory, the continuities of meaning and judgment are also lost to us.” That diagnosis seems even truer today, as the functions of remembering, judging and interpreting are increasingly offloaded from human minds and communities into the ether of social media feeds and artificial intelligence. We’re in danger of forgetting how to look. “A conversation with the NYT critic on his popular close reads of poems.” “Nature writing after everyone stopped paying attention.” “Today, photo geeks the world over consider Paul Kwilecki a master of the documentary form.”

  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Vincent Koebel/NurPhoto via Getty Images) “Mamadou” was clearly itching to get straight to business when he sent a message on Snapchat to one of the young organizers of the recent student protests that have seen high schools across France blockaded and thousands of teens taking to the streets to demand more funding for education. The “student,” who sported an Algerian and Senegalese flag alongside his name on Snapchat, wanted to break some things — everything, in fact. And to be added to the group where such delinquency might be discussed. “Bismillah, c’est les ténèbres,” his message begins, “In the name of God, it is the darkness.” The message continues in a mashup of North African Arabic and French slang that I will try to reproduce as faithfully to the tone as possible here: “When’s the blockade I swear my homies. We’re gonna break everything. Add me to the blockade group, thank you,” followed by two Algerian flag emojis. The short message was so full of absurdities, anachronisms and racist assumptions that the recipient knew at once it wasn’t from a fellow student. It was, they suspected, from the police, trying to infiltrate their organizing network and conjuring their own ideas of how kids talked and behaved to do it. Later reporting from La Provence indicated they weren’t too far off: The geolocation of the original message indicated that it likely came from a police station in Aix-en-Provence, the news site reported. Rather than dismiss it, the students have transformed the nonsense phrase “Bismillah, c’est les ténèbres” into its own shibboleth — and something akin to the great Steve Buscemi “How do you do, fellow kids” meme of the 2010s. It has appeared on placards and banners at protests across the country; student leaders are using it as a rallying cry; even Greta Thunberg invoked it in a recent video supporting the movement. Beyond the meme, however, is the reality of the way the French state views its young citizens , particularly those of Arab, Maghrebi and African descent. The issue that drove students to the streets — underfunded schools where buildings are falling apart and teacher absenteeism is rampant — impacts Black and Arab neighborhoods at higher rates. As does aggressive policing . * The French Far Right Has Opened Pangram’s Box
  New Lines | 20 Oct 11:06 UTC
  When an anonymous X account alleged that the it-book of the year in France was likely AI-generated, the scandal threw the literary world into an existential crisis. But under the veneer of integrity lay deep connections to the far right. The post The French Far Right Has Opened Pangram’s Box appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: The question was posed at 4:39 p.m. on Monday, Sept. 21, by @Pangramed, an anonymous X account with just 92 followers. It was accompanied by a picture of Thélyson Orélien’s hit debut novel, “It Was Either That or Die” (“C’Était Ça ou Mourir”). Since its French release in August, the novel and its 38-year-old Haitian-born Canadian author have taken the literary world by storm. The story of a schoolteacher who flees gang rule in Haiti and crosses 12 countries to reach Canada, the novel was the book of the “rentrée littéraire,” France’s avalanche of new fiction for the autumn literary season. It had already won two prizes, was shortlisted for the Goncourt — France’s most prestigious literary prize, awarded to the likes of Marcel Proust and Simone de Beauvoir — and had seduced legions of readers. Among them was the mayor of Beziers, Robert Ménard, who has often been accused of turning his city into a laboratory for the far right. He said it brought him to “rethink his view of migrants.” But the X post wasn’t another glowing review from a newly converted reader. It was the undoing of a career on a meteoric rise, in a matter of 1,200 characters. The novel was, the post claimed, “written almost entirely by artificial intelligence.” @Pangramed brought what they claimed were the receipts: screenshots from Pangram, an AI-detection program the account described as “the most reliable” on the market, suggesting that “almost 100%” of the book had been written by AI. Newsrooms across the political spectrum fed “It Was Either That or Die” into other AI detection sites, with conflicting results. For Pangram’s false positive rates, it should be noted, are not negligible. The company estimates that its overall false positive rate is 1 in 10,000, though other factors, including the language of the text, can affect that rate. Within days, the Goncourt Academy nonetheless struck the novel from its shortlist. The Orélien scandal, as it would come to be known, quickly took France by storm. Mainstream coverage focused obsessively on existential questions around the future of literature, with AI-detection tools held up as the now unavoidable sentinels of the (human-)written word. As a safety measure, the other 15 books shortlisted for the Goncourt were fed into Pangram as well. According to the president of the Goncourt Academy, these novels “passed the test with flying colors, with the exception of a few ridiculous dregs” which have, unlike “It Was Either That or Die,” remained un
  https://newlinesmag.com/argument/the-french-far-right-has-opened-pangrams-box/

* The Longreads Questionnaire, Featuring Jasper Craven
  Longreads | 20 Oct 10:00 UTC
  He wrote the book on how the military shapes masculine identity. He'll also tell you about Disney adults, Pharaoh Sanders, and his favorite spot for a burger and negroni.
  FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, during an appearance on Kareem Rahma’s popular Subway Takes show, Jasper Craven explained line-crossing ceremonies, in which sailors celebrate first crossings of the equator. In US naval history, line-crossing ceremonies have at times involved violent hazing. But they have also involved pageantry, ritualistic performances of masculinity and femininity that tip over into drag. “Young men want love, support, community,” Craven said. “The military provides a permission structure to show love, often in very pure ways.” It’s a characteristic insight from Craven, a freelance reporter who has made a singular beat from the intersection of masculinity and the American military. In his magazine features, Craven has scrutinized Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s opposition to women in the armed forces via Hegseth’s undergraduate fits over The Vagina Monologues , and profiled Bernarr McFadden , a bodybuilder and publisher who oversaw two military schools “to impose his love of physical strength onto American children, the War Department, and the world.” Craven’s recent book, God Forgives, Brothers Don’t , follows some of our most insidious models of masculinity back to the military education programs that have shaped them for centuries. Craven is a dynamic counterexample to the men he writes about, less interested in bulking up the boundaries of his identity than testing their limits. For a memorable Harper’s feature, he spent six months undercover as an employee of the world’s largest private-security firm. (During his first shift, a supervisor criticized Craven for his non-detachable necktie, which could be used to strangle him.) He is at once stridently critical and deeply compassionate. (He is also, as you’ll learn, deeply and truly funny.) “There are myriad other ways to prove your manhood, and they’re not simply being emotionally vulnerable or non-violent,” Craven told an interviewer earlier this year. “You can actually fight for things: fight for peace, be a little risky, be a little reckless, break the law, put your body on the line. If it’s around a morally sound mission, that can feel incredibly validating.” I don’t take a lot of heart from the men Craven writes about, but I take some heart from his work.
